So the basic definition of empowerment is essentially it's a measure of the potential information that an agent can inject into the environment by its actions and pick up later by its sensors. And it has multiple interpretations. There are many converging ways of looking at that. It's a it's a way of of maximizing your vitality, maximizing your options to run away from death, which is zero empowerment. We're with William Gabert who will be presenting on NGCLARN V3. You may remember a previous symposia where William presented as well. So this will be great to catch up and hear about this toolkit and some of the cool things that people can do to apply and do modeling in it. So thank you William to you. >> Yeah. Hi. So my name is William Gart. Um I am representing the neuro adaptive computing laboratory from the Rochester Institute of Technology in New York. Um I'm here today to talk to you about NGCLearn specifically the V3 which is our upcoming release of NGCLearn. Um it is a fast and modular computational neuroscience library. Uh and with the acquisition of V3 it just gives you a bunch of new tools to work with. So first off I just want to give a very brief overview on what computational neuroscience actually is. So computational neuroscience at a glance is basically just the study and investigation of functions of the brain uh brain functions using mathematical computational tools and theories. Uh and what this means is that uh instead of going uh to math first and then trying to claim that we are you know using things like the brain uh we go to the brain first and then try and model the processes that happen in the brain with math. Um your brain is not actively doing a bunch of matrix multiplication but if you look at it from a high enough level and squint properly it does look like it's doing matrix multiplication and so we can approximate things that way. Um, and we are very focused on the bioplausibility of all of this. Um, and it's all grounded very much in the physical studies of living organisms. Um, so there are studies that look at how neuropathways work. There are studies that work at how synaptic plasticity works. There are studies that look at memory. Uh, and these are all just the various things that we look at in computational neuroscience. Uh now what this kind of means is that we need tools to work on this. Um because the brain is complex. Uh for example, if when our brain is interpreting the world, uh it is receiving lots of data um like gigabytes and gigabytes and gigabytes of data coming in constantly. And what this means is that if we have a model, we need to uh obviously we're just going to be looking at a small paired down version of the amount of data that comes into our brain. So we're always working with a slightly suboptimal picture. But on top of that, your brain isn't ever idle. So in traditional deep neural like deep learning, uh you take an image, you feed it into your model, and then you get an output. Um, sometimes we do things what's known as recurrent neural networks where you give it an input, it goes and it processes and then you give it another input and it uses the output of the previous one and the new input and it processes. That's closer to what your brain does. But the problem there is all of the little internal parts of that network don't have values. Um, and so like we want to make sure that we're representing things with biology. Sorry, was there somebody speaking? Okay, sorry. Um, and so then the this biological interpretation also lends itself to how we get inputs into our models. Uh, so for instance, the focal point of your human eye can only really process things at roughly 60 frames a second. Uh, your periphery processes things a lot faster than that, but the focal point doesn't process much faster than that. Um, but even in between those frames, it doesn't sit idle. Um and then obviously different sensors in our bodies uh impact uh sense things at different time. For instance, the periphery, the uh rods in our eyes process things a lot faster than the cones in our eyes. Um, and so then we work inside of this biological framing that I was alluding to earlier where when we model our neurons in our when our when we model our neurons uh we want to make sure that they are stateful and that we are working in a more uh time aare system. Um, and we do this because your brain is a time system. And so we still use a lot of the similar words that you're going to hear in traditional deep learning. But for us when we say we are talking about a syninnapse, we are talking about the actual modeled connection of the syninnapse. Uh when we're talking about a neuron, we are talking about voltages, currents, membrane potentials. We are talking about the physical biological aspects of a neuron. And what this also means in our biological framing is that the way that information is transmitted from uh point A in our network to point B in our network. Um it can't just send a floating point number because your brain doesn't send floatingoint numbers. The way neurons communicate in our brains is with uh pulses. Uh for we call them spikes. And so this in and of itself makes things a lot more complicated when it comes to modeling because if we're sending discrete pulses over time, we have to do a full forward pass over our model to figure out where pulses happen, send pulses, and then we have to do that again and again and again and again. And basically what it looks like at the end of all of this is we have about a 200 layer recurrent network where it's just going down for like 200 time steps. But really it's not a recurrent network. It's just constantly processing new information and constantly giving you some output, but that output might just be null. So all of this comes together to basically say that everything works better as just one giant state machine. So we have our model, we're calling it S. And then when we take a step in time, we move to another state. And depending then on what we do and in time, either we can move down, left, right, whatever. And these states are constantly evolving. And most active inference models, a lot of just computational neuroscience models in general, they all have this stateful uh framework that they work in of where if you just pause the entire model's processing of data, there's always something going like there's always values that exist. Um and the main thing to keep in mind with all of this is that these different initial conditions and state initial starting state uh vastly change the model's output even if it's shown the same information. So that's kind of the overview of computational neuroscience state for modeling and kind of setting up our problem space. Now this is not a like this isn't a field that came about 2 years ago. This is a field that people have been working and tinkering around in for a little bit of time now. Um, so that might be the question of well, somebody probably has built a built something before and they have. Um, I'm going but the thing about building uh previously built models is that they kind of generally operate as black boxes. Um, so because building these large stateful systems incur incurs a lot of overhead, most libraries that exist today hide a lot of what's going on. and you have some very specific tools that you can use to uh modify the internals of your system, but you don't necessarily have all the tools that you want. If you want to come up with a new learning rule, maybe you don't have all of the values that you need for that learning rule, and there's no good way of modifying the system internally. And so testing new components and everything is not easy. It's not trivial. It's something that takes a fair amount of time. And if we're testing models that take a long time to run, it means that if we're it can you can just lose days, weeks to just trying to get a model to see if it'll do anything and you don't know whether or not it'll work. Um, and so what the, and just as a reference point here, many some of these alternative models take up to like 8 hours to do an epoch over emnest for a moderatelysized spiking neural network, which is a lot. Um, and so this is kind of why we went to like our lab uh went to building our own computational neuroscience library. And so that is called ngcarn. That is what I'm here to talk about today. And basically this is the little highlights reel and then we'll get into exactly how it's working. But it is a public pip package. You today right now could go and type in pip install ngcarn um and you will get a version of ngcarn. I will tell you right now you're going to get 2.0 which is a perfectly functional version but it will not look exactly like what I'm talking about today. Um, ngcarn at its core though is a state machine that sits next to uh Jax which is a it's effectively the linear algebra library for numpy uh that was the backbone of TensorFlow before it split. Um, and Jax allows us to do things that are known as just in time compilation. uh which allows us to take a bunch of Python code that we know is has fixed sizes and fixed types and just produce effectively a C code snippet that we can run on GPUs, TPUs, CPUs, whatever. Um and in doing this our models in some preliminary results you can get from something that takes about eight hours written in TensorFlow to something that takes about 15 minutes on the same hardware written in ngcarn. Um and then the advantage of this is that you can also make it also allows for the creation of new methods, new models, new components, whatever incredibly easily because as long as you follow a very simple coding paradigm, everything will interface with it perfectly. So let's get into what some of that looks like at a high level. So if we're going to build a model in ngcarn, we do have to slightly change how we are thinking about modeling a network specifically. It's for us it is just one giant state machine. But if we just treat it as one giant state machine, it gets very hard to keep everything organized and not end up putting values where they're not supposed to be or just, you know, moving all the data around. So we use components inside of our state machine which here we're calling a context. We use components to kind of hold parts of the state uh together and modify only a sub part of our state and then be able to communicate with other components which just also encompass a different part of our state. And this whole compartment uh sorry component model uh just allows for us to keep easy track and it's basically bookkeeping that allows the end user to say I'm going to take these same values and do something new with them. Similarly inside of a component we have a compartment model and this is where data is stored and is moved around. Um and this is what allows us to very easily move data from one component into another component uh without a lot of overhead. So more specifically uh com looking at components here here we have one for what is known as the leaky integrate and fire neuron. Now specifically the math that happens inside of this isn't particularly relevant. Uh but know that this is one of the most common neurons used in computational neuroscience for modeling. Um what it is is it is effectively a uh it's effectively a capacitor where it slowly builds up current uh passes a threshold discharges emits a spike. Now what this means is that there are some fixed parameters. If we look here the number of unit the number of neurons in our like layer of uh leaky integrate and fire neurons is fixed. We're not growing new neurons magically as this is going. uh the that voltage threshold that it's slowly building up to that's also fixed. Uh we have some time constants, we have a resistance value and we have some voltage rest and reset values. These are all fixed parameters. These don't change in time. You set them up once at the beginning of your model and then you just call run. Now they can vary um from component to component but as far as time is concerned they are fixed. Then we get to the compartments which are the variable parts in time of our component. So here we have current as an input into our component. At a various time we will have a current. Similarly if we pause at a different time we will have a voltage and at that same voltage maybe we have an output spike and we also have the last time that we spiked. So we keep a track of all of these values that change in time. And this allows us to think about our components in two steps. We have here's every we have here's all of the math and the logic and the fixed parameters. And then here we have a couple of values that we need to make sure are updated at the end of every time we are called. So that way we keep up to date with our state. Finally, how do we connect these together? So if we have component A and we have its parameters which are our fixed values and we have our compartments which are our variable values and then we have component B which has different parameters and different compartment values but we want to take the output of component A and we want to make sure that it gets put as the input to component B. Oh, sorry. Uh that just means that you're just going to take the value here and it just gets passed along and it's done and it go and it's traverses along what is known as a cable. And so inside your model, you can take any compartment value and you can wire it with a cable into any other compartment value. And what that means is that when component B looks for the value inside this compartment, it will just know that it actually should be looking at the value inside this compartment. And so this is how that we can keep things nice and fast is there's no duplicates of values but effectively it traverses along it. Now with component X Y and Z down here we're doing something a little special. We have a summation. So now it's entirely reasonable to assume that the inputs into our component uh need like we could have a variable number of inputs. Uh, one of the best examples of this is if you have a recurrent connection inside of your model um that are both feeding into say the current value of your LIF neuron. What that means is that you would have going down your model, you would have the current coming in and then you have current from somewhere up in your model also looping around. And basically there we might want to add these together. We might want to subtract them together. We might want to have them multiplied and have some basic arithmetic operation done to them. And so we allow you to do that along these cables is you can basically bundle these cables together and then just say hey add all of these together and you can do that and this compartment here knows how to do all of that. So when you go and try and see the value of this compartment it will automatically collect these values add them together and use it. And you as the end user don't have to make sure that that happens. And if for instance we unplug component Y from the model, it just runs. If we add in a component alpha to our model that we want to add to this summation, we just add it into we just wire it into the uh we just wire it into the compo compartment like anything else. It all just works together. Now, this shows us how to set up our model, but we're a state machine, so we actually have to be able to transform through states. So, transitioning our model through time. So, here we have our state transitions across a component. We're going to look at these at a specifically a component level and then we'll look at them as as a whole system as our model. Uh here we have component A. uh it has our fixed parameters like before and here we have two different state transitions that we want to account for. Then since these are wired together, the when this when when we call advance over here on component B, it'll actually look at the output of component A and use it for component B. Greetings everyone. My name is Siddhant and I'll walk you through our recent work on enhancing bisantine tolerant formation control for maritime buoy swarms using you guessed it right active inference. What are maritime buoy swarms? As you can see on my screen maritime buoy swarms are a swarm of buoys like deployed in the ocean in order to achieve certain tasks. For example, if there is an oil spill or um like um an incident in the ocean, these buoys help in monitoring that. They also help support missions like surveillance, search and rescue and other kinds of environmental monitoring in hostile or contested waters. The threats that we focus on in this particular work rely on the configuration that you see in front of you. All of these buoys can be imagined as decentralized robots floating in the ocean with minimal motor control and they can communicate among each other and a centralized hub. But because of this they are also prone to cyber physical attacks. So we will focus on three kinds of attacks which can impact their operation and mission success. So for example, if the task is area coverage like taking down one or more of these buoys is equivalent to a bisonantine problem where we cannot trust certain buoys anymore and now we have to measure how to measure the mission success in this particular uh scenario so that the rest of the buoys can reorganize around this and in order to do do that they need to detect the cyberphysical attack at play. So the threats that we will focus on are GPS poofing, communication jamming and actuator hijacking. Why these three? Because these three push the resilience limits of existing control architectures which may not use artificial intelligence or any active inference approach. Our goal in this work is to maintain formation integrity and task coverage while detecting and mitigating malicious agents. I'll start with our baseline approach and then explain how we integrated active inference, where it shines, where it still struggles, what alternatives we considered, and how we plan to push the results further. The original meth methodology without active inference uses a decentralized neighborto- neighbor approach in order to facilitate communication. Each buoy assessed trust using hand-crafted manual heruristics, rules that downweighted agents exhibiting inconsistent trajectories, communication dropouts or mission throughput anomalies. We combined that multi-dimensional trust score with an attack aware formation controller which essentially helped reate the neighbor influence based on trust estimates while enforcing formation geometry and coverage. This approach was practical and interpretable and it handled many different kinds of anomalies well as you can see in the graph in front of you. However, it lacked formal uncertainty quantification which is very important for this kind of a task. Strugg it struggled with subtle or staged attacks that we uh inflicted upon the system and sometimes even over penalized honest agents during complicated maneuvers. In our improved methodology like we see we see that we introduced an active inference-based trust assessor. The core idea here is to move from uristic rules to a probabilistic generative model and variational basian inference. Each buoy in this new model maintains an internal state predicting neighbor trajectories position, velocity and acceleration and even jerk to measure change in momentum. While taking into account estimated formation forces when reality deviates from predictions, we compute a mahalenobis distance across those dimensions. High prediction errors uh will suggest potential tampering. We then update our trust beliefs using basian filtering explicitly tracking both the mean trust score and its uncertainty. The formation controller uses those uncertainty aware trust estimates to moderate neighbor influence. Agent communication stays decentralized. The key change is that the trust updates now come from predictive modeling rather than fixed thresholds. And this as you can see improves the detection accuracy a bit across all three metrics. Where active inference is useful can be highlighted in the following ways. Like the graphs are pretty clear that active inference over a period of time can help in improving the results that we are seeking. But to do that it helps mostly in three areas. First is early detection. Predictive errors surface anomalies before they manifest as gross deviation. We see this particularly with GPS spoofing position when they diverge sharply from expected trajectories. Secondly, uncertainty quantification helps us in carrying variances along with the trust means so that we can pro protect the honest agents when the model is unsure. This reduces false positives during tight maneuvers. Thirdly, it helps with attack specificity. Different attack modes produce distinct error signatures which are important for us to quantify and measure. GPS poofing elevates the position residues. Communication jamming shows up in missing observations. Actuator hijacking disrupts velocity and jerk. We calibrate these responses accordingly. In our V2 results, this translated into an 8.4% average improvement in detection accuracy, a 1.6% boost in resilience, and 6.6 6% gain in task coverage with actuator hijacking coverage jumping nearly 20%. And the results are pretty clear. We do not claim that active inference is always the better approach. We can see here that active inference does a lot better at task coverage and resilience score whereas it is nearly um as performant in other metrics or sometimes less performant in average trust score over time. Where active inference still falters is that the generative model still simplifies the hydrodnamics and control inputs. When cooperative agents execute aggressive maneuvers, prediction error spike and the system can result in false alarms. Moreover, communication jamming creates data gaps that challenge the inference updates. Even with tempered thresholds, detection improvements for actuator hijacking um like are minuscule. So what do we do? Um we see that um detection improvements are modest with only 3.5% because actuation dynamics are hard to model at the granularity we need. So active inference is computationally heavier for these decentralized forms with lesser processing budgets. So we need reliable processing power in order to ensure real-time updates which may not always be a solution in a maritime environment. The alternative approaches that we have considered was a reinforcement learning based trust estimation where agents learn policies from simulated attacks. It offers adaptability but lacks the interpretability and uncertainty guarantees that we wanted. Another was a consensus based filtering with fall tolerant observers. Classic control theory techniques. These have strong stability proofs but require centralized coordination or strict communication assumptions. Another thing that we could look at is an hybrid approach which combines calman filters with heristic gating which shows um which has the potential to show some promise yet um like it also has the potential to lack explicit uncertainty handling. What active inference gives us is a balance between theory interpretability and decentralized operation. The key finding for us is that active inference can outperform huristic baselines across detection resilience and coverage while providing principled uncertainty estimates. GPS poofing detection jumped over 15%. Uh our communication jamming detection um improved by 7%. And actuator hijacking coverage improved almost 20%. These are not basic numbers. These are quite significant. So how do we go about it in a practical setting? We confirm that these protective floors and attack specific calibrations are essential. Without them, active inference overreacts. Finally, the predictive modeling gives the commanders richer diagnostic signals. Not just what went wrong, but how confident the system is in that assessment. This is where active inference really shines. We can improve upon a few things as next steps. Our next steps focus on richer generative models incorporating better hydrodnamic parameters for this environment, external disturbance introduction and learned control inputs. We plan to fuse communication reliability signals more tightly with behavioral predictions to handle jamming gaps. We also want to explore hierarchical active inference where local agents share compressed summaries of their beliefs to enhance collective awareness without breaking decentralization. Finally, we plan to benchmark against adaptive consensus and reinforcement learning hybrids to ensure that we are harnessing the best aspects of each approach. In summary, active inference has pushed our maritime swarm resilience forward in a simulation environment, giving us predictive confidence and measurable improvements against bisonantine threats. The work is not done. We need to do a lot more to move this from a Python-based simulation environment to the real world. But the path is clearer. We need to sharpen the models, strengthen the communication inference loop, and validate under more diverse operational profiles. Thank you. I'm a neuroscientist working at the Max Frank Institute for Human Cognitive and Brain Sciences and at the Okinawa Institute for Science and Technology. I study how people come to feel Today, I want to talk about a problem that's obvious to anyone who has ever been in a therapy room. Mental health interventions depend on attunement. When therapist and client are not aligned, therapy stalls or even fails. Yet, we have almost no quantitative tools for monitoring attunement in real time. My colleagues and I propose a way forward. We build on active inference models of social interaction and link them to a geometric hypers scanning observable that is suitable for clinical deployment. We will also sketch a prospective digital twin for psychotherapy that uses real-time monitoring of neural geometry to mark and eventually predict when crucial behavioral events say ruptures, co-regulation and reatunement occur. The common scientific approach to social cognition is to treat one person as an observer trying to infer the hidden mental state of another. This third person stance assumes social understanding happens through detached one-way observation. In contrast, second person neuroscience emphasizes that meaningful interaction is embodied and reciprocal. When two people engage, their brains form a coupled generative system. Computationally, each partner maintains a generative model of their own as well as of the other person's states. predictions are continuously updated and belief updating becomes mutual. This dissolves the regress of I model you modeling me modeling you into a shared dynamic narrative and this framework the diet not the isolated individual is the basic unit of A effect fits naturally into this picture. Emotional balance can be formalized as inference over the coherence of a self model. Two quantities drive it. The magnitude of self-model prediction error. So how surprised you are about yourself and the self-relevance of that error. So how much the situation touches your identity. We include an effective memory term to account for emotional inertia and allow different temporal orientations. prospective, retrospective, fast, slow, intense or smoother, which we call temporal aim. The position parameters alpha and beta, which you'll see in our um paper, tune the influence of surprise and memory. In a diatic interaction, prediction errors become entangled because each agent recursively models the other. Positive balance signals smooth coordination whereas negative balance signals breakdowns within active inference. A effect acts as a soft prior that biases policy selection towards trajectories that minimize expected surprise. Therapists sometimes use their intuition to sense when the alliance with a client is strong or weak. In social neuroscience, hyperscanning allows us to monitor brain activity simultaneously in multiple people. Most hyperscanning studies compute correlation or phase locking metrics which describe neurosynchrony but cannot tell us when and how the network reorganizes. These metrics treat interbrain coupling as somewhat static and misdynamic topological reconfigurations associated with rupture and repair. Network geometry offers a more mechanistic lens. Discrete geometric notions like the form and Ricky curvature quantify how information expands or contracts across a network. In a weighted network, positive curvature marks edges embedded in dense redundant clusters while negative curvature marks bridging edges that connect different modules. This edge centered measure captures local flow in ways that nodebased synchrony can't. Our method which we've dubbed geometric hyperscanning computes the formal rickety curvature on time resolved interbrain networks derived from dual EEG fur or fMRI recordings. We treat each cortical region as a node and functional connectivity as weighted edges for each edge connecting nodes I and J. The formal Ricky curvature is computed over the weight of that edge multiplied by the sum of one divided by the weight of node I and 1 divided by the weight of note J. Then you subtract the correction term which is the sum over all edges that share a note with the edge under consideration of the ratio of that edg's weight to the square root of the product of that edg's weight and its neighbors weight. This context the function w assigns weights to nodes and edges. To track how the overall geometry evolves, we compute the entropy of the curvature distribution within a sliding window. Curvature entropy at time t is calculated by taking the negative of the sum over all edges present at that time of the probability of each edge multiplied by the natural log of that probability. Here the probability of an edge is the curvature of that edge divided by the sum of curvatures across all edges. This resulting scalar curvature entropy measures the volatility of interbrain topology. Abrupt peaks or discontinuities in curvature entropy indicate topological phase transitions in the shared generative manifold. When the entropy spikes, it often corresponds to effective rupture. When it declines, repair and reatunement are underway. How do the how do we use these insights in therapy? Each partner in therapy, so the therapist and the client is modeled as an agent whose self model contains a generative model of the other. Prediction errors in this recursive model manifest as topological changes in their combined neural activity. By combi by computing former Ricky curvature on the weighted interbrain network this third brain between therapist and client and tracking the entropy of its distribution. We obtain a realtime readout of when the interaction is stable, rupturing or repairing. Here's where the idea of a digital twin becomes powerful. A mental health digital twin is a virtual representation of an individual's mental states that is continually updated with data collected over the person's interaction. Such a twin can guide professionals in diagnosing and treating patients using mechanistic models, machine learning tools. The digital twin concept extends beyond simple simulations. It spans the neurohysiological cycle of an individual and updated by real world data using physical and statistical models to answer what if questions in mental health. Digital twins could forecast deterioration, predict treatment outcomes, and recommend tailored interventions. Implementing this framework requires robust data in careful modeling. Hypers scanning signals must be pre-processed to construct reliable time resolve functional connectivity matrices. The curvature calculation depends on appropriate waiting schemes. Positive curvature highlights redundant local clusters while negative curvature highlights bridges. Entropy should be computed within windows suitable for the modality's temporal resolution. shorter windows for EG or longer ones for fMRI. In real clinical settings, the digital twin must integrate behavioral cues, gaze, procity, gestures, and self-report data with the neural one. Each of these streams can inform the posterior over latent states, ensuring the twin is grounded in multimodal evidence. Ethically, digital twins raise issues of privacy and agency. Despite these challenges, the potential benefits are considerable. By embedding geometric hypers scanning within an active inference framework, we can develop social markers, realtime signatures of interpersonal coordination analogous to biomarkers. in therapy. These could alert practitioners to ruptures they might otherwise miss, guide decisions about when to pause or reframe an intervention, and help quantify progress over sessions. In research, curvature-based metrics allows allow us to test hypotheses about how belief updating and effective revelation unfold moment by moment. In technology, digital twins could inform adaptive interfaces and social robots that adjust their behavior based on the geometry of human interaction. Mental health interventions thrive on attunement. Yet quantifying attunement has remained elusive. We propose a unified framework that models therapist client interactions as coupled generative systems, formalizes affect as inference over selfmodel coherence and measures interbrain dynamics using discrete geometry. The formal ricky curvature and its entropy reveal dynamic phase transitions, rupture, cogulation and reatunement which standard synchrony measures miss. Building on this insight, we sketch a digital twin for psychotherapy, a virtual entity that integrates real time neural, behavioral, and emotional data to timestamp and anticipate shifts in relational state. Such a twin has a potential to assist therapists in guiding sessions towards the optimal outcome, namely healing. We invite collaborations to test this approach on real clinical data and further refine the models for broader use. Thank you. Okay, we're back. So the idea is that um if someone is speaking to you and um you rather than if it if something arises that maybe you disagree with or there's some uncertainty about you seek to understand the other person before you add your own opinion. So today we're going to use um a structured form of communication and we're joined by Susan, Andrew, and Leonardo to demonstrate this. And there are um certain stages to it, but the general principles are that when you're listening to someone speaking, then you uh seek to understand what they're sharing through reflecting what you've heard back to them. And if there's an error, it gives the person who spoke a chance to clarify or they may want to add further detail. So the mindset we have to approach is to be comfort to be comfortable with the error and um if you are sharing then be um open to revealing your whole experience. So um that could be more the effective side of the experience. So uh your emotional veilance as well as just the facts. So um I propose that this type of approach um can help with generative model alignment in transis transdisciplinary research. So um through this turn taking in small groups we can continually update our beliefs about others intentions and perspectives. It can help with precision calibration. So uh the practice can highlight which signals deserve more attention and which need revisiting. It can help with epistemic action and shared anticipations over time. So a quick um bit of detail about what is involved. So we imagine we're sitting in a virtual circle. And then after then they add their own opinion about the topic. So their fresh opinion about the topic. It doesn't have to be related to what the previous person said. Then this continues around the circle. So person the third person then takes the role of the seeker. So seeking to understand and they reflect what person two has said and that continues. All right, next pre-recorded video is going to be Sanjie Nam joi fundamentals of active inference a self-guided textbook for learning and applying active inference from first principles. This is going to be a 20inut video. Hello, my name is Sanjie Namjoshi and I'm a machine learning engineer working at verses. If you've ever tried to learn active inference, you probably know what it feels like to get lost in a maze of subdisciplines. Neuroscience, information theory, Beijian statistics, physics, and more. Every paper seems to assume you already know three other fields with different mathematical notations. equations seem to materialize out of nowhere with few good resources explaining where they came from or how they're even useful in the first place. Today, I would like to talk to you about my my progress toward providing greater accessibility, visibility, and knowledge of active inference and the free energy principle. I'm excited to announce that my textbook fundamentals of active inference is complete and will be released in March 2026 from the MIT press. My aim for this textbook is to provide a unified, systematic and rigorous foundation for students and researchers entering the field to reduce the steep learning curve. However, an important motivating question is why should you learn active inference in 2025? To answer this question, I think it is helpful to turn the clock back 2006 and draw a comparison between the state of deep learning in its early days and active inference as we understand it today. Prior to the mid-200s, neural networks were just one of many possible models rather than the dominant choice in academia and industry. This all changed in 2006 when Hinton and his colleagues released the deep belief network paper which is generally understood as the start of deep learning as we understand it today. After some various hardware innovations and the release of the well-known imageet library, we started to see more coverage and success of AI in the news. But in 2012, deep learning truly provided its value with AlexNet. And for the first time, it achieved better than human performance on image detection tasks. But this was just the start. What followed was a proliferation of deep learning across industry and research. I've added some well-known milestones just to highlight the explosion of progress in deep learning in the last decade. But there is so much more that deserves mention and attention here. And since I presented about my textbook at the 2023 active inference symposium, the state of AI has dramatically changed once again. Today, large language models dominate the AI landscape and have managed to overshadow many other fields and alternative approaches. New and more powerful language models are released every quarter, though the rate of advancement over previous models is beginning to slow. So what about the state of active inference as a field? From my perspective, active inference lies in the same position as deep learning did in 2006, influential and on the brink of exploding in popularity and relevance. This paper here um from which this figure comes from is from contributors at the active inference institute and shows the current growth of publications in the active inference community. In the last few years, active inference as a field has seen a number of important milestones. Here I show just a few that broaden the scope and attention to the field. We have the first international workshop on active inference in 2020. We have the first applied active inference symposium and the foundation of the active inference institute. We have the release of the par pizzazzulo and fristen 2022 book as well as the pmdp library. And from 2023 to present there are so many pieces and changes here. Uh some of which include new Julia packages RX infer um and many many more papers in robotics and other industries as the international workshop of active inference has begun to grow and grow and grow. I see active inference as perfectly poised for greater visibility and attention, especially given the current interest in generative AI and deep reinforcement learning. Working alongside the active inference institute and other organizations, my aim here is to provide some of the fundamental materials to capture the attention and notice of machine learning researchers and students to bridge this gap to bring active inference into its renaissance. So why learn active inference in 2025? Well, active inference is highly interpretable. The very nature of Beijian networks means that you can follow the chain of reasoning through a particular problem to see why a certain um outcome was chosen over others for a particular model, why a particular policy was chosen. For example, active inference comes equipped with a principled modeling system, a way to model any system of interest using an elegant and all-encompassing encompassing formalism based in information theory. Active inference also has a more robust interpretation of agency than current LLMs as we talk about agency in a lot of industry contexts today and includes an aspect of control of the external environment. Active inference is fundamentally compatible with the notion of world models that capture the cause effect understanding of the external world and it's believed these world models um are very important for pre preventing hallucinations that we see in large language models. Active inference can also be used to solve everyday practical problems in research and in industry. And it may also expand the field of complex systems modeling which is central to fields such as physics, climate science, economics, political theory, psychology, sociology, and more. Active inference also provides a convenient way to univise unify many disperate fields that are exploring the same ideas in different languages or scientific conventions. And although it's been yet to be fully demonstrated, active inference does have the potential to be more cost and sample efficient at scale. And more research needs to go into this area, which is why we need a new generation of active inference researchers uh coming to contribute to the field. Due to the current state of active inference and its potential as a premier AI research framework, my aim is to expand the reach of active inference beyond its current confines. To this end, I've aimed to make my textbook as accessible for the widest audience possible with the least amount of required prerequisites. The major focus is writing this book for an engineering audience. Both machine learning engineers and traditional engineering disciplines. Neuroscience is largely out of scope for this book and there are many other resources available if you're interested in that particular aspect of the free energy principle and active inference. Many of the recommended and even optional prerequisites that you see here are typically known by undergraduate students in science and engineering and certainly by graduate students in these fields. Nevertheless, the book is also written to be readable for those that desire to focus on everything. The completionist that wants to know all the math, the concepts, and the code. It's also suited for people who are more interested in the theory. uh maybe just interested in the math and concepts to get a general sense of things so they can dive into the literature further. And my hope is that ultimately um those who are intuitionists who want to skim over the math and just get a sense of the ideas should still be able to read this book. And ultimately I hope to convince you by reading this textbook that active inference may be challenging but it is not impossible to learn by any means. Part one of the book called fundamentals will set the stage by describing the statistical setting with an agent interacting with its environment. We start as simple as possible with a single state and a single data point and then we begin to expand from there. Connections are made in chapter 3 to other classical parameter estimation techniques in statistics as well as a complete investigation into the basics of variational basian inference which happens in chapter 4 where variational free energy is also introduced. The field of predictive coding is also explored in part one which we can see as a kind of precursor one of the precursors to the modern formulation of active inference. And this will also allow us to introduce the notion of prediction errors which are ubiquitous in the active inference and also the neuroscience literature. Part two, the active inference core now introduces the core active inference methodologies. Chapters 6, seven and 8 focusing on continuous state spaces and chapters 9 and 10 uh focusing on discrete state spaces. Like part one, we start simple in the simplest possible model we can imagine and then begin to grow and build up the model to the full vanilla active inference agent step by step. Part three, applications and extensions cover various developments that have uh occurred in the last uh 8 to 7 8 to 10 years uh in the field which include um modifications and extensions to base active inference and also a number of other topics included here like structure learning, factor graphs, robotic applications, different types of free energies, hybrid discrete and continuous models, the free energy principle and so on. There are also extensive appendices covering the history of the field and its connection to other fields, a deep overview of all notation in the book, a uh exhaustive summary of the different forms of variational free energy you might find in the literature, and a least you need to know for all background mathematical topics in active inference. Um, you can see here that there's a lot of coverage that gives you the basic details. So even if you're not an expert in all of these different background topics, you have enough of a sense of how to actually understand why they're relevant in the context of active inference and understand when they show up in various papers of interest. So now I'd like to give you a preview of what's contained in the textbook itself. Type setting and style is extremely important to successful learning. So I've spent a lot of time attempting to make my work clear and readable. Margins are used for footnotes and supplemental figures uh that can aid reading comprehension. There's a large focus on building intuitive understanding of concepts. For example, all algorithms are explained from scratch. And in this book we uh start from a description of the agent environment modeling problem. Describe the univariate case extend the multivariate case. Then we introduce variational inference. Add dynamics. Add generalized coordinates if applicable. Uh we look at hierarchical models and we also look at action building on it step by step and then looking also at learning and attention as well to see how they fit into the broader framework. Figures are designed to clearly walk the reader through the text and give detailed visualizations of important concepts. There are also concept maps provided for most chapters which lay out the fundamental vocabulary that will be covered in the chapter and show how they're all interrelated to one another. And this should hopefully form a useful guide as the different sections are also numbered and shown in the concept map so you can understand where that topic comes from and how it relates to something else that you may have read earlier in the text. The early part of the book focuses on basic concepts such as hidden state estimation that is estimating the conditional distribution of a latent variable given observed data. And the aim here is to explain the modeling paradigm in the context of an agent attempting to infer the states of the environment. This is an interaction between a generative model and a generative process. And this is a perspective that differs from the way that Beijian inference is normally taught. In chapter 3 and also in the code notebooks that I'll talk about in a moment, we go through this perspective taking something as simple as linear regression and putting it into the context of an agent environment interaction to really make those concepts clear and understandable. Typically, introductory Beijian inference books focus on parameter estimation or what we call learning in this book. And in part one, I introduced the expectation maximization algorithm to make it clear what the relationship is between classical parameter learning and estimation techniques that you might see in a basian context in certain textbooks and this notion of hidden state inference. We deal with latent variable models to understand how they connect together within one algorithm which sets the stage for the active inference models that come later. Variational inference is also explained in detail. The book features a catalog of all the different forms of variational free energy or expected free energy in the literature and how they can be derived from one another and how the different forms interrelate. The book also covers raw and Ballard style predictive coding terms and ideas such as prediction error minimization as well as a clear explanation of fundamental concepts such as surprisal and variational free energy. I'm also making extensive use of Bashian networks in this book so that all models can be seen graphically and we also build up starting simple to more and more complex models throughout the book. Factor graphs which are an alternative way of representing Beijian networks that have a number of other interesting and very useful properties um are explained in chapter 12 where I go through all the different models we've seen so far in the book and show how they can be represented as a factor graph to build that intuition and understanding of how these kinds of models work. There are more than 230 custom figures that have been created for the book, most of which are full color. And the figures are extremely detailed to give the reader a deep understanding of the content that I provide in the book. And many of the models presented in the book in the book are also shown in pseudo code which would aid in implementation. My aim for readers is to go through these algorithms and be able to implement them in code yourselves. Although code will also be provided for those who also want to learn through reading the notebooks and running pre-existing code.
